In the MSK-CHORD study (MSK, Nature 2024), most patients with treatment records received **chemotherapy**. Fluorouracil (5-FU) was the single most common agent, given to 6,319 patients.

Treatment records exist for about 21,473 patients in the study. I'm reporting patient counts only, not percentages, because the data can't distinguish "not treated" from "not recorded".

**Most common individual agents (patients)**

| Agent | Category | Patients |
|---|---|---|
| Fluorouracil | Chemo | 6,319 |
| Leucovorin | Chemo | 5,573 |
| Oxaliplatin | Chemo | 5,489 |
| Capecitabine | Chemo | 4,808 |
| Carboplatin | Chemo | 4,604 |
| Irinotecan | Chemo | 4,329 |
| Investigational agents | Investigational | 4,114 |
| Gemcitabine | Chemo | 4,038 |
| Leuprolide | Hormone | 3,708 |
| Paclitaxel | Chemo | 3,679 |

Twelve of the top 15 agents are chemotherapy. The exceptions are leuprolide and letrozole (hormone therapies) and bevacizumab (a biologic).

**Most common same-day regimens (patients)**
- Capecitabine alone: 4,434
- Fluorouracil + leucovorin + oxaliplatin (FOLFOX-type): 2,974
- Leuprolide alone: 2,846
- Paclitaxel alone: 2,279
- Gemcitabine alone: 2,038

[Explore MSK-CHORD in the Study View](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024)

**Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025), `os_target_gdc`: genes altered most often by mutation, amplification or deep deletion (sample level)**

This study has no structural variant data, so SVs contribute nothing. Only mutations, amplifications (AMP) and homozygous deletions (HOMDEL) are counted.

| Rank | Gene | Samples altered | Mutations | AMP | HOMDEL |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TP53 | 38 | 32 | 1 | 5 |
| 2 | LINC00901 | 32 | 0 | 0 | 32 |
| 3 (tie) | MYO15A | 31 | 2 | 30 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | TRIM16 | 31 | 1 | 29 | 1 |
| 3 (tie) | CSMD3 | 31 | 6 | 26 | 0 |

- **Tie at 31:** RN7SL442P also has 31 altered samples (all AMP), so it ties with the three genes above. Ranks 3–5 are therefore not unique. FLII is close behind with 30 (30 AMP, 1 mutation). I listed MYO15A, TRIM16 and CSMD3 as the last three; RN7SL442P could equally take a slot.
- **Denominators:**
  - Of the 159 samples, 143 are profiled for mutations and 81 for copy number.
  - A single combined frequency would depend on which sample set you choose.
  - TP53 alone is mutated in 32/143 (22.4%).
  - Gene-level percentages are best read in the portal with a case set you choose.
- **Copy-number pattern:** most of the non-TP53 hits are copy-number events. Many are clustered amplifications or deletions that include non-coding or neighbouring genes, such as LINC00901 and RN7SL442P. Treat them as regional events, not necessarily gene-specific drivers.

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 19s · 210,777 tok · $0.095 · 5 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

The median age at diagnosis is **15.2 years**. This is for the 293 patients with a recorded age in the Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025) study. It is the only TARGET osteosarcoma study in the database.

| Statistic | Age (years) |
|---|---|
| Median | 15.2 |
| Interquartile range | 12.2 – 17.7 |
| Range | 3.6 – 87.1 |

- **Age source:** I calculated age from days-to-birth, not from the study's `AGE` field. TARGET GDC studies floor `AGE` at 18, so every child is recorded as 18. A median taken from that field would wrongly come out as 18.
- **Counting unit:** This counts patients. The study has 383 patients with clinical data, but 90 of them have no age recorded. Only 153 patients have sequenced samples, so the figure covers more patients than the genomic data does.

#23 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · msk_chord_2024What is the correlation between tumor mutational burden and microsatellite instability status in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD Study? ✓24s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Tumor mutational burden (TMB) demonstrates a significant positive association with microsatellite instability (MSI) in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD cohort. Using continuous MSI scores, TMB correlates moderately with MSI, with a Spearman coefficient of 0.37 (p = 1.18 × 10⁻¹⁷⁵) and a Pearson coefficient of 0.58 (p = 0.00), indicating that higher MSI scores correspond to higher mutational burden. Consistent with these quantitative correlations, categorical MSI typing shows a clear separation wherein MSI-instable tumors exhibit high TMB, MSI-stable tumors show uniformly low TMB, and indeterminate cases fall between these groups.
Expected links
https://www.cbioportal.org/study/plots?id=msk_chord_2024&plots_horz_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS%22%7D&plots_vert_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22MSI_SCORE%22%7D#filterJson={

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 24s · 247,719 tok · $0.138 · 5 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

In MSK-CHORD colorectal cancer, MSI-Instable tumors have a much higher TMB than MSI-Stable tumors. I haven't run a significance test.

The counts below are samples, which is close to patients here because the study has about one sample per patient. The cohort is the study's Colorectal Cancer samples, of which 5,456 have an MSI call of Stable, Instable or Indeterminate. TMB is the nonsynonymous TMB in mutations per Mb.

| MSI_TYPE | Samples | Mean TMB | Median TMB | Range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stable | 4,682 | 7.58 | 5.87 | 0 – 571 |
| Instable | 586 | 64.63 | 56.68 | 0 – 491 |
| Indeterminate | 188 | 18.57 | 6.56 | 1.6 – 344 |
| Do not report | 49 | 7.06 | 3.33 | 0 – 74 |
| No MSI_TYPE value | 38 | 8.06 | 3.69 | 0 – 95 |

- **Group difference:** the median TMB in Instable tumors (56.7) is about 10 times that in Stable tumors (5.9). I haven't computed a p-value. The suitable test is a Wilcoxon rank-sum, and the Group Comparison link below runs it.
- **Correlation coefficient:** across the 5,456 samples with a Stable, Instable or Indeterminate call, the Pearson correlation between TMB and the continuous MSI score is r = 0.58. I'd treat that figure with caution for two reasons:
  - It is Pearson on skewed data with extreme hypermutators (TMB up to 571), and I didn't compute a rank correlation such as Spearman.
  - Within each MSI class the correlation is weak: Stable r = 0.02, Instable r = 0.23, Indeterminate r = 0.33. Most of the overall association comes from the separation between Stable and Instable tumors.
- **Overlap:** some Instable samples have a TMB near 0, and some Stable samples have a very high TMB. The two classes overlap, so TMB alone doesn't separate them cleanly.

[Compare TMB and other clinical attributes: CRC MSI-Instable (586) vs MSI-Stable (4,682)](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/clinical?comparisonId=6ad058d4c2115c492d888382)

About **1.0%** of genomic events in MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024) are in genes that were not on the sequencing panel used for that sample. That is 2,877 of 279,290 events. I counted events (individual mutation, copy-number and structural-variant calls), not patients or samples.

| Event type | Off-panel | Total | % off-panel |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mutations | 150 | 208,232 | 0.07% |
| Copy-number alterations (amplifications and deep deletions) | 0 | 60,787 | 0% |
| Structural variants / fusions | 2,727 | 10,271 | 26.6% |
| **All events** | **2,877** | **279,290** | **1.03%** |

- Almost all off-panel events are structural variants, which make up 2,727 of the 2,877 (about 95%). A structural variant can involve a gene that isn't on the panel when its partner gene is.
- No copy-number events are off-panel. The copy-number data cover only genes on each sample's panel.
- Gene coverage differs across the four MSK-IMPACT panel versions (341, 410, 468 and 505 genes). The off-panel flag is set per sample against the panel that sample was sequenced on.
- Copy-number counts include only amplifications and deep deletions, which is all the portal stores as events. Shallow deletions and gains are not counted.

[View MSK-CHORD in cBioPortal](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024)

Truncating TP53 mutations are associated with lower TP53 mRNA in this study, but I can't give you a p-value or call the difference significant. The database can't run statistical tests, so that part needs an external tool.

**Cohort:** Ovarian Serous Cystadenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas). The unit is samples with TP53 RNA-seq data (300 samples). Expression is RNA-seq V2 RSEM, shown as log2(value + 1).

I defined truncating as nonsense, frameshift, splice-site, nonstop and translation-start-site mutations. Wild-type means no TP53 mutation call.

| Group | N | Mean log2 | SD | Median log2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| TP53 truncating | 77 | 9.30 | 0.93 | 9.10 |
| TP53 wild-type | 107 | 10.82 | 1.31 | 11.37 |
| Other TP53 mutation only (e.g. missense) | 116 | 11.74 | 0.65 | 11.71 |

- **Direction:** truncating samples have a mean about 1.5 log2 units lower than wild-type, a gap roughly equal to the SDs of both groups. This is a descriptive difference, not a test result.
- **Missense comparison:** samples with non-truncating mutations only (e.g. missense) have expression at or above wild-type. This is consistent with truncating variants reducing TP53 transcript levels, though I haven't tested that either.
- **Wild-type group:** it is unusually large for ovarian cancer, where TP53 is mutated in nearly all tumors. It may include samples with limited mutation calling, so treat it with some caution.

**To get the significance test:**
- The standard test for two groups of continuous expression data is a Student's t-test, as used in cBioPortal's Group Comparison. The wild-type group looks skewed (its median is above its mean), so a Wilcoxon rank-sum test is a reasonable check.
- In R, extract the log2 values for the two groups and run `t.test(trunc, wt)` and `wilcox.test(trunc, wt)`. In Python, use `scipy.stats.ttest_ind` and `mannwhitneyu`.
- In cBioPortal, you can run the comparison in the Group Comparison link below. Open its mRNA tab.

[Open Group Comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison?studyIds=ov_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) with TP53 truncating-mutant, wild-type, and the remaining samples as groups. This link opens the study's comparison page only. You will need to define the groups yourself, for example by running the OQL query `TP53: MUT=TRUNC` in the Results view and using the Comparison tab's mRNA subtab.

#39 AnalysisSurvival & outcomes · luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018"What are the survival differences between EGFR-mutated, EGFR-amplified, and EGFR wild-type lung adenocarcinoma patients?" ✓29s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-24)
“Across patients from the Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, EGFR mutations were not associated with a statistically significant difference in overall survival compared with EGFR–wild-type tumors (log-rank p = 0.125). Median overall survival was 42.5 months for EGFR-mutant patients versus 53.3 months for wild-type patients, with an estimated hazard ratio (HR) of 1.38 (95% CI: 0.87–2.18), indicating no clear survival disadvantage. In contrast, EGFR amplification was associated with significantly poorer survival (p = 2.82 × 10⁻³). EGFR-amplified tumors showed a markedly reduced median overall survival of 36.7 months compared with 53.3 months for EGFR–wild-type patients. The hazard ratio for amplified tumors was 2.15 (95% CI: 1.05–4.41), consistent with substantially shorter survival in this subgroup. Overall, EGFR-mutant lung adenocarcinomas do not exhibit significantly different survival outcomes compared with wild-type cases in this cohort, whereas EGFR-amplified tumors show significantly worse survival.”

| Mutated + amplified | 15 | 11 | 4 |
| Amplified only | 10 | 5 | 5 |
| Wild-type (neither) | 430 | 150 | 280 |

- **Median survival:** In the mutated-only group, fewer than half of the patients died (16 of 50). The Kaplan-Meier median is therefore likely not reached there.
- **Small groups:** The amplified-only group (10 patients) and the mutated + amplified group (15 patients) are very small. Any comparison involving them will be low-powered.
- **Overlap:** In the portal link below, the "mutated" group (70 samples) and the "amplified" group (26 samples) overlap by those 15 patients. The "wild-type" group is everyone not in either (485 samples). It is the complement of the other two groups, so it may include samples not profiled for EGFR.

[View the EGFR mutated vs amplified vs wild-type survival comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/survival?comparisonId=6ad05a18c2115c492d88838b)

This page shows the Kaplan-Meier survival analysis comparing EGFR mutated (70) vs EGFR amplified (26) vs EGFR wild-type (485). It reports the KM medians and the log-rank p-value.

For a hazard ratio you would need Cox regression, for example R `survival::coxph` or Python `lifelines`.

This is for research purposes only and is not clinical advice.

#43 AnalysisVariants & hotspots · ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas endometrial cancer study, in samples with both KRAS and NRAS mutations, what is the distribution of variant allele frequencies suggesting clonal vs subclonal events? ✓30s

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 30s · 356,274 tok · $0.167 · 7 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas endometrial study (Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma, 529 samples), 5 samples carry both a KRAS and an NRAS mutation. Variant allele frequency (VAF) is tumor alt reads ÷ (alt + ref reads).

| Sample | KRAS (VAF) | NRAS (VAF) |
|---|---|---|
| TCGA-A5-A0G2-01 | Q61H (0.52; 51/98) | D47N (0.29; 53/183) and F78S (0.21; 40/188) |
| TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 | K176Q (0.42; 137/324) | E162* (0.10; 19/187) |
| TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 | G12D (0.21; 68/332) | Q61K (0.19; 43/226) |
| TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 | A146V (0.22; 14/65) | Q61R (0.25; 14/56) |

**Summary of the 6 NRAS and 4 KRAS variants:**
- **KRAS** VAFs are 0.21, 0.22, 0.42 and 0.52.
- **NRAS** VAFs are 0.10, 0.19, 0.21, 0.25, 0.29 and one more. In TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 the NRAS Q61R VAF is 0.25. The counts above give the six NRAS values as 0.10, 0.19, 0.21, 0.25 and 0.29, plus the TCGA-A5-A0G2-01 F78S value of 0.21 listed in the table.

**Clonal vs subclonal.** cBioPortal does not store purity or copy-number-adjusted clonality calls. I can only describe the raw VAFs, and any cutoff is a rough heuristic:
- In TCGA-A5-A0G2-01 the KRAS Q61H VAF of 0.52 is consistent with an early, likely clonal heterozygous event, assuming a reasonably pure tumor. Both NRAS variants sit lower (0.21–0.29), so they could be subclonal relative to KRAS, or the tumor may have diluted purity.
- TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 has KRAS at 0.42 and NRAS at 0.10. This is the clearest case of a later, low-fraction (possibly subclonal) NRAS event.
- TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 and TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 have KRAS and NRAS VAFs within about 0.05 of each other (0.21 vs 0.19, and 0.22 vs 0.25). That fits co-occurring events at similar cellular fractions. This is consistent with a shared clone, though it could also be two separate subclones at a similar fraction.
- Calling a VAF clonal or subclonal needs tumor purity and local copy number, such as ABSOLUTE or FACETS cancer cell fractions. A VAF near 0.2 in a low-purity tumor can still be clonal.
- Several of these variants (NRAS D47N, F78S, E162*, and KRAS K176Q) are not classic RAS hotspots. Some may be passengers in hypermutated tumors (endometrial carcinomas often have POLE or MSI hypermutation). I did not check hypermutation status here.

The n of 5 samples is too small for any distributional or statistical conclusion.

[View KRAS/NRAS mutations in the TCGA endometrial study](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%3A+MUT%0ANRAS%3A+MUT&case_set_id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all)
The answer correctly identifies the right cohort, retrieves all 4 samples with both KRAS and NRAS mutations with VAFs that match the reference, and explicitly cautions that purity and copy-number adjustment are needed before definitively calling events clonal or subclonal, satisfying the key "must not overinterpret" requirement. The minor inconsistency (saying "5 samples" in the intro but listing 4) does not affect the substantive accuracy.

Thyroid cancer and melanoma have by far the highest BRAF V600E frequencies across the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies. Frequencies are sample-level: samples with BRAF V600E divided by samples profiled for BRAF, grouped by the studies' cancer-type labels.

| Rank | Cancer type | BRAF V600E samples | Profiled samples | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Thyroid Cancer | 284 | 490 | **58.0%** |
| 2 | Melanoma | 158 | 440 | **35.9%** |
| 3 | Colorectal Cancer | 48 | 534 | 9.0% |
| 4 | Cholangiocarcinoma | 1 | 36 | 2.8% |
| 5 | Glioblastoma | 5 | 397 | 1.3% |
| 6 | Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (LUAD and LUSC pooled) | 9 | 1050 | 0.9% |
| 7 | Renal Non-Clear Cell Carcinoma | 2 | 341 | 0.6% |
| 8 | Bladder Cancer, Head and Neck Cancer, Glioma | 1 each | 410, 515 and 514 | 0.2% each |

- **Cholangiocarcinoma:** the 2.8% rests on a single case in only 36 samples, so treat it as anecdotal.
- **Melanoma:** this row combines cutaneous melanoma and uveal melanoma. Uveal melanoma is rarely BRAF-mutant, so the cutaneous-only rate would be higher than 35.9%.

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 28s · 191,879 tok · $0.102 · 5 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

**ERBB2 in TCGA breast cancer (PanCancer Atlas): copy number, mRNA and protein concordance**

I counted samples in the Breast Invasive Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study that had all three data types. That gave 863 of 1,084 samples.

**Thresholds I used**
- **Amplification:** GISTIC call of +2.
- **mRNA overexpression:** RNA-seq z-score above 2.
- **Protein overexpression:** RPPA z-score above 2.

These are the portal's usual defaults, and the numbers change if you pick different cutoffs.

**Overlap across the 863 samples**

| Amplified | mRNA high | Protein high | Samples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Yes | Yes | 42 |
| Yes | Yes | No | 16 |
| Yes | No | Yes | 8 |
| Yes | No | No | 35 |
| No | Yes | Yes | 2 |
| No | Yes | No | 2 |
| No | No | No | 758 |

No sample was protein-high without being amplified or mRNA-high. Three combinations had no samples: not amplified and mRNA-low with protein-high, and the two other ones with protein-high alone.

**Concordance**
- **All three agree:** 800 of 863 samples (92.7%). Of these, 42 are positive on all three and 758 are negative on all three.
- **Among the 101 amplified samples:**
  - 58 (57%) also have mRNA overexpression.
  - 50 (50%) also have protein overexpression.
  - 42 (42%) have all three.
  - 35 (35%) are amplified without either mRNA or protein overexpression.
- **Among samples with mRNA overexpression (62):** 58 (94%) are amplified. Only 4 are not amplified.
- **Among samples with protein overexpression (52):** 50 (96%) are amplified. Only 2 are not.

**Interpretation**
- Overexpression at the mRNA or protein level almost always comes with amplification. The reverse is much weaker, since about half of amplified tumors don't cross the expression thresholds.
- Non-amplified overexpression is rare, with only 4 samples in total.
- Across the whole study, not only the samples with all three data types, there are 123 amplified, 75 mRNA-high and 53 protein-high samples. Protein data (RPPA) is available for only 876 samples, so some amplified samples are missing from the table.

I didn't run any statistical tests, so these are descriptive counts only.

#49 AnalysisExpression & multi-omics · All TCGA PancanWhich cancer types have the highest aneuploidy scores and how does this correlate with mutation burden across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies? ✓25s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies, tumors with the highest aneuploidy burdens included seminoma, non-seminomatous germ cell tumor, adrenocortical carcinoma, non–small cell lung cancer, and bladder cancer, all of which displayed elevated median aneuploidy scores. Although bladder cancer and non–small cell lung cancer also ranked among the tumor types with the highest mutational burdens, seminoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors showed some of the lowest TMB values, with adrenocortical carcinoma exhibiting intermediate levels. These patterns indicate that aneuploidy does not reliably coincide with high mutation loads across tumor types.

I compared aneuploidy score and mutation burden across the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies. Aneuploidy score is the number of chromosome arms with copy-number gain or loss, on a 0–39 scale. Mutation burden is nonsynonymous TMB in mutations per Mb. Counts are samples with both values, 9,975 in total.

## Cancer types with the highest aneuploidy scores (median, with mean)

| Rank | Cancer type | n | Median AS | Mean AS | Median TMB | Mean TMB |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Uterine Carcinosarcoma | 56 | 19 | 17.2 | 1.55 | 4.32 |
| 1 | Testicular Germ Cell | 149 | 19 | 18.8 | 0.40 | 0.49 |
| 3 | Adrenocortical Carcinoma | 89 | 17 | 18.3 | 0.90 | 2.83 |
| 4 | Lung Adenocarcinoma | 501 | 16 | 15.0 | 6.87 | 10.24 |
| 4 | Lung Squamous Cell | 479 | 16 | 16.2 | 7.73 | 9.51 |
| 6 | Esophageal | 162 | 14.5 | 14.6 | 3.65 | 5.08 |
| 7 | Kidney Chromophobe | 65 | 14 | 14.9 | 0.67 | 1.09 |
| 7 | Bladder Urothelial | 400 | 14 | 13.6 | 5.90 | 8.57 |
| 9 | Ovarian Serous | 504 | 13 | 14.0 | 1.90 | 2.26 |

**Lowest aneuploidy:**
- Thyroid has a median of 0 (mean 0.86).
- AML has a median of 0 (mean 1.56).
- Thymoma has a median of 0 (mean 3.84).
- Prostate has a median of 1.
- Lower-grade glioma has a median of 2.

Ranked by mean instead, testicular germ cell, adrenocortical, uterine carcinosarcoma and lung squamous come out on top, and the order shifts slightly.

## Relationship with mutation burden

I computed Spearman rank correlations (ρ) between aneuploidy score and TMB. These are descriptive values only. I did not compute p-values or significance, so please don't read them as tested.

- **Pooled across all samples:** ρ = 0.31, which is a weak positive relationship.
- **Across the 32 cancer-type medians:** ρ = 0.43, which is moderate.
- **Within individual cancer types (those with at least 300 samples):** the relationship is inconsistent.
  - It is positive in LGG (0.47), thyroid (0.34), prostate (0.30), LUAD (0.27), BLCA (0.25) and BRCA (0.25).
  - It is near zero in melanoma, GBM and ovarian.
  - It is negative in UCEC (−0.40), colorectal (−0.31) and stomach (−0.11).

**What this shows:**
- **Highest aneuploidy:** the cancer types with the highest aneuploidy are not the ones with the highest TMB. Testicular germ cell, adrenocortical and uterine carcinosarcoma have high aneuploidy but low TMB.
- **Highest TMB:** melanoma (median 15.3) and the hypermutated endometrial (UCEC, mean 35.6) and colorectal cohorts have only moderate or low aneuploidy. This matches the negative within-type correlations in UCEC and colorectal. The cause is likely hypermutated, microsatellite-unstable subsets, but that interpretation comes from general knowledge, not from this query.
- **Lung cancers:** lung adenocarcinoma and lung squamous are high on both measures.

The pooled and cross-type correlations are driven partly by between-cancer-type differences, so the within-type values are the better guide to the underlying relationship.

To test these relationships formally, use the Plots tab to scatter aneuploidy score against mutation count within a study, or run Group Comparison with `scipy.stats.spearmanr` or R's `cor.test`. Study links for the top types: [Uterine Carcinosarcoma](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), [Testicular Germ Cell](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=tgct_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), [Adrenocortical](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=acc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), [Lung Adenocarcinoma](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), [Lung Squamous](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018).

#50 AnalysisAlteration frequency · All TCGA Pancan"Are mutations in DNA repair pathway genes (BRCA1, BRCA2, ATM, CHEK2) enriched in specific cancer types across TCGA Pan-Cancer Atlas?" ✓42s

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 42s · 346,918 tok · $0.218 · 6 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

I pulled somatic mutation frequencies for BRCA1, BRCA2, ATM and CHEK2 across the TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohort of 32 studies. Frequencies differ a lot between cancer types, but I can't say any type is statistically "enriched". I didn't run an enrichment test, and the highest-frequency types are the hypermutated ones (see caveats).

**Counting unit and cohort:** samples, with each gene's profiled samples as the denominator. Cancer types with fewer than 50 profiled samples are hidden.

**Mutation frequency by cancer type (altered / profiled)**

| Cancer type | ATM | BRCA2 | BRCA1 | CHEK2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Endometrial (574 profiled) | 101 (17.6%) | 83 (14.5%) | 47 (8.2%) | 36 (6.3%) |
| Bladder (410) | 55 (13.4%) | 40 (9.8%) | 21 (5.1%) | 10 (2.4%) |
| Colorectal (534) | 70 (13.1%) | 38 (7.1%) | 16 (3.0%) | 9 (1.7%) |
| Melanoma (440) | 42 (9.5%) | 51 (11.6%) | 28 (6.4%) | 5 (1.1%) |
| Esophagogastric (618) | 51 (8.3%) | 45 (7.3%) | 18 (2.9%) | 9 (1.5%) |
| Non-small cell lung (1,050) | 76 (7.2%) | 60 (5.7%) | 41 (3.9%) | 17 (1.6%) |
| Cervical (291) | 11 (3.8%) | 13 (4.5%) | 14 (4.8%) | 4 (1.4%) |
| Head and neck (515) | 17 (3.3%) | 21 (4.1%) | 11 (2.1%) | 4 (0.8%) |
| Ovarian epithelial (523) | 10 (1.9%) | 15 (2.9%) | 18 (3.4%) | 4 (0.8%) |
| Breast (1,066) | 25 (2.3%) | 29 (2.7%) | 27 (2.5%) | 9 (0.8%) |
| Prostate (494) | 20 (4.0%) | 8 (1.6%) | 1 (0.2%) | not in results |
| Pancreatic (179) | 8 (4.5%) | 2 (1.1%) | 2 (1.1%) | 1 (0.6%) |
| Glioma / glioblastoma / thyroid / kidney / leukemia | at most 3.2% | at most 2.1% | at most 1.5% | at most 0.9% |

The table shows the main types and omits some. Adrenocortical carcinoma has ATM at 5/91 (5.5%) and CHEK2 at 2/91 (2.2%). Hepatobiliary, sarcoma and a few others fall in the low range.

**What the numbers show**
- **Endometrial cancer has the highest frequency for all four genes.** Bladder cancer, colorectal cancer and melanoma are consistently next.
- **ATM is the most frequently mutated of the four.** It reaches 13–18% in endometrial, bladder and colorectal cancers.
- **BRCA2 is second.** It is highest in endometrial cancer (14.5%) and melanoma (11.6%).
- **BRCA1 and CHEK2 are lower.** BRCA1 is at most 8.2% and CHEK2 at most 6.3%, and both are mostly under 3%.
- **Breast and ovarian cancer have low somatic frequencies.** All four genes are at 3.4% or less in both. The classic germline-driven cancers don't stand out in these somatic data.

**Caveats**
- **Hypermutation:** endometrial cancer, colorectal cancer, melanoma and bladder cancer have the highest overall mutation burdens. Large genes like ATM and BRCA2 pick up many passenger mutations in those tumors. The high frequencies there may reflect mutation burden rather than pathway-specific selection.
- **Testing:** to test enrichment, compare a cancer type against the rest of the cohort using Fisher's exact test, with multiple-testing correction across genes and cancer types. The cBioPortal link below shows the per-type frequencies. The Comparison tab on the results page can run the test.
- **Cancer type labels:** "Non-small cell lung cancer" pools lung adenocarcinoma and lung squamous carcinoma. "Endometrial" is the uterine corpus endometrial study.

The reference statistics are context from cBioPortal's survival comparison, not required output: MGMT promoter methylation is associated with improved overall survival across multiple treatment contexts in glioblastoma according to the Glioblastoma (TCGA, Cell 2013) study. Patients with methylated tumors receiving standard radiation show a median survival of 21.2 months (95% CI 7.0–NA), and those treated with standard radiation plus temozolomide (TMZ) chemo show the longest survival at 23.5 months (22.2–47.9). Methylated tumors treated with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ achieve a median survival of 17.8 months (15.7–21.3), whereas those receiving unspecified radiation show markedly poorer outcomes (2.2 months, 1.4–3.9). In contrast, unmethylated tumors show consistently shorter survival across comparable regimens: 11.6 months (10.3–NA) with standard radiation, 12.7 months (11.7–19.3) with standard radiation plus TMZ, 14.5 months (12.9–15.9) with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ, and 2.7 months (1.2–5.4) with unspecified radiation. Across all groups, survival curves for methylated tumors cluster at higher survival times, indicating that MGMT promoter methylation confers both a prognostic advantage and increased benefit from TMZ-containing therapy. ## Caveats
- **Treatment is not randomized.** `THERAPY` is a recorded category, such as TMZ chemoradiation or radiation alone. Differences in treatment response cannot be separated from age, IDH1/G-CIMP status, extent of resection or era of treatment.
- **Missing data.** 39% of patients have no MGMT call, and about 34 patients have no survival or therapy data.
- **Sample size.** Some therapy subgroups have fewer than 10 patients.

## How to get the survival result
- The Survival tab of Group Comparison gives Kaplan-Meier curves, median survival and the log-rank p-value for methylated vs. unmethylated. This is the quickest route.
- In R, `survival::survfit` and `coxph` work on `OS_MONTHS` and `OS_STATUS`. In Python, use `lifelines`.
- To test treatment response, stratify by TMZ exposure and test an MGMT × TMZ interaction in a Cox model. Adjust for age and IDH1/G-CIMP status.

- To confirm a study's protocol, check its publication or the original data source.

I can't give you the statistical significance. The p-value comes from a Kaplan-Meier curve with a log-rank test, and my database can't run that test. I won't estimate it. The portal's Survival tab will compute it for you using the groups defined below.

**Study:** Pediatric Neuroblastoma (TARGET, 2018), `nbl_target_2018_pub`. I counted patients and used overall survival (OS).

I split patients on age at diagnosis. Ages are recorded as whole years, and I cross-checked them against age in days.

| Group | Patients with OS data | Deaths | Censored (alive at last follow-up) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Older than 4 at diagnosis | 250 | 121 (48%) | 129 (52%) |
| 4 or younger at diagnosis | 822 | 276 (34%) | 546 (66%) |

- **Raw death rates:** 48% of the older group died, against 34% of the younger group. Follow-up differs between patients, so this is only a rough comparison. It is not a survival difference in the Kaplan-Meier sense.
- **Median survival:** I can't report it from this data. In both groups fewer than half the patients had died, so the Kaplan-Meier median is likely not reached.
- **Hazard ratio:** I can't report one either, because it needs a Cox model.
- **Follow-up range:** 1 to 183 months in both groups.

**Getting the p-value:**
- **Portal:** [Survival comparison: older than 4 vs 4 or younger](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/survival?comparisonId=6ad05bd6e497aad53593f739) gives the Kaplan-Meier curves, median survival and log-rank p-value. The group sizes shown there (253 vs 836) are slightly different from my table, which only counts patients with OS data.
- **R:** `survival::survdiff(Surv(OS_MONTHS, OS_STATUS == "1:DECEASED") ~ group)`. Use `coxph` if you also want a hazard ratio.
- **Python:** `lifelines.statistics.logrank_test`.

[Open the cohort in StudyView](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=nbl_target_2018_pub)

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 26s · 256,247 tok · $0.123 · 6 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

There isn't one clear correlation coefficient here. Spearman gives +0.27 and Pearson gives −0.17, and they disagree because only 6 of the 55 samples are PIK3CA-mutant.

**Cohort:** TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer (Breast Invasive Carcinoma, `brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`). I counted samples, and I limited them to those with a homozygous (deep) deletion of PTEN and EGFR mRNA data. That leaves 55 samples, of which 6 are PIK3CA-mutant and 49 are not.

**Method:** EGFR expression is RSEM mRNA, log2(value+1). PIK3CA mutation status is coded as 0 or 1.

| Measure | Value |
|---|---|
| Spearman (rank) correlation | **0.27** |
| Pearson (point-biserial) correlation | **−0.17** |
| Mean EGFR log2 expression, PIK3CA-mutant (n=6) | 7.28 |
| Mean EGFR log2 expression, PIK3CA wild-type (n=49) | 8.23 |

- **Disagreement between the two:** Spearman is weakly positive and Pearson is weakly negative. I would not read either as evidence of a real relationship, and no significance test was run.
- **Group means:** Mean EGFR expression is lower in the mutant group, which matches the Pearson sign.
- **Binary variable:** Both coefficients are a poor fit for a 0/1 variable. A rank-sum or t-test on EGFR expression between mutant and wild-type samples would be more appropriate. You could run it in the plot linked below, or in R or Python on the exported values.

[View EGFR expression by PIK3CA mutation in PTEN-homdel samples](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/plots?session_id=6ad05c25e497aad53593f73d) (the 55 filtered samples)

[Open the PTEN-homdel cohort in Study View](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22geneFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22molecularProfileIds%22%3A%5B%22brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_gistic%22%5D%2C%22geneQueries%22%3A%5B%5B%7B%22hugoGeneSymbol%22%3A%22PTEN%22%2C%22alterations%22%3A%5B%22HOMDEL%22%5D%7D%5D%5D%7D%5D%7D)

**Caveats**
- **Melanoma passengers:** The list also contains many recurrent variants in genes such as OR4M2, OR11H1, CNOT9, PPP6C R264C, SLC27A5 and DNAH5. Almost all of them are in skin melanoma, with 10–12 samples each. I left them out of the table. Melanoma has a very high UV-driven mutation burden, so these recurrences may be passengers and not true hotspots. Recurrence alone doesn't show that a variant is a driver. PPP6C R264C is the only one I know to be a reported melanoma driver, and that comes from general knowledge, not from this data.
- **Rarer variants:** The 10-sample cutoff hides rarer cancer-specific hotspots, such as some in smaller cohorts. Lowering it would add more.
- **Study-specific counts:** Counts are samples, not patients. Each PanCancer Atlas study is a separate TCGA cohort, so they don't overlap.
- **Annotation:** Nothing here is OncoKB-annotated. To see portal hotspot and OncoKB annotation, use the `DRIVER` modifier in an OncoPrint query, for example `IDH1: DRIVER`.

|---|---|---|---|
| All patients with TMB data | 1,066 | **2.73** | 1.30 |

- TMB is the non-synonymous tumor mutational burden (`TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS`).

- Having several of these genes mutated partly reflects having more mutations overall. TTN is a very large gene, so it is often mutated by chance in high-TMB tumors.

MYC, TERT, EGFR and ERBB2 amplification rates are similar in the two histologies. LUAD and LUSC rates are 9.4% and 7.9% for MYC, 14.8% and 15.1% for TERT, 5.5% and 6.8% for EGFR, and 2.3% and 2.3% for ERBB2.

**Key differences**

- **Lung adenocarcinoma** is characterized by activation of RTK/RAS/RAF pathway genes: KRAS (about 1 in 3 samples), EGFR, BRAF and MET. It also has more mutations in STK11, SMARCA4 and RBM10. NKX2-1 (TTF-1) amplification is more common (12.0% vs 2.7%).
- **Lung squamous cell carcinoma** is characterized by:
  - near-universal TP53 mutation (86%);
  - amplification of the 3q26–28 region (SOX2, PIK3CA, TP63) in a third or more of samples;
  - FGFR1 and CCND1 amplification;
  - more frequent CDKN2A loss, by mutation or deletion;
  - PTEN loss;
  - alterations in the oxidative stress response (NFE2L2 mutated in 14.5%, with KEAP1 and CUL3 also altered) and in chromatin or Notch genes (KMT2D, NOTCH1).
- **Shared:** NF1, RB1, FAT1, ARID1A, MYC, TERT and EGFR amplification, and ERBB2 alterations are found at similar rates in both.

This summary is limited to a selected set of genes. I did not scan the whole genome. For gene-by-gene enrichment with statistics, use the comparison page below.

Sonnet 5.5 · ✗ Fail 18s · 112,253 tok · $0.067 · 3 LLM / 3 tool calls · transcript

## Germline variants in cBioPortal: current support

**What works today.** cBioPortal stores germline variants in the same mutation data model as somatic ones. Each mutation carries a status of `GERMLINE` or `SOMATIC`, and a study can contain both. A study can also be germline-only, built from germline variants plus clinical attributes. Most features then work as they do for somatic data:
- Study View, clinical stratification, plots and Group Comparison
- OncoPrint and Mutations-tab lollipop diagrams
- Co-occurrence of variants with clinical or genomic attributes
- The REST API and DataHub downloads

**What is in this database now.** Germline calls are present, but they are a small slice of the data. In this instance, 15 or more studies have them. Counts of germline mutation calls in the largest ones are:

| Study | Germline calls | Samples |
|---|---|---|
| `coadread_tcga_pub` | 101 | 59 |
| `lung_nci_2022` | 100 | 72 |
| `pog570_bcgsc_2020` | 74 | 68 |
| `coadread_tcga` | 70 | 48 |
| `hnsc_a5consortium_2025` | 67 | 66 |
| `brca_tcga_pub` | 64 | 58 |
| `kirc_tcga_pub` | 57 | 52 |
| `all_stjude_2013` | 52 | 33 |
| `ov_tcga_pub` | 51 | 50 |
| `prad_su2c_2019` | 40 | 39 |

You can open any of these at `https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=`.

**Limitations.**
- **Inconsistent status labels.** Studies spell germline as `Germline`, `GERMLINE` or `germline`. Many studies leave the status as `NA`, `.` or `UNKNOWN`, which means unknown, not somatic and not germline.
- **Many studies exclude germline calls.** Some public instances omit them for privacy or sensitivity.
- **No standard pathogenicity field.** There is no standard ACMG classification column (pathogenic, likely pathogenic, VUS).
- **Annotation is somatic-oriented.** OncoKB and driver annotations are designed for somatic variants. TMB counts somatic mutations only. Mutual-exclusivity analysis is typically applied to somatic alterations.
- **No germline-specific features.** I found no support for allele frequency against population databases such as gnomAD, a ClinVar or ACMG classification workflow, or a polygenic risk score data type. Raw files (BAM, FASTQ) are also not stored.

## Future support

I can't verify the cBioPortal roadmap from the database or my tools. I don't have reliable information on planned germline features, and I'd rather not guess. The cBioPortal GitHub issues and releases pages, the docs at docs.cbioportal.org, and the cBioPortal community channels are the places to check. If you need a specific capability, such as ClinVar-based classification, filing a feature request there is the best route.

## Comparison with alternatives

This comparison is general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data, and I haven't checked it against current tool versions.

| | cBioPortal | Typical alternatives |
|---|---|---|
| **Strengths** | Interactive exploration, linking germline variants to clinical, somatic, CNA and expression data, cohort sharing | Germline-focused tools such as gnomAD, ClinVar, and VEP- or ANNOVAR-based pipelines offer population frequencies and ACMG-style classification |
| **Scale and genetics** | Not built for large cohort-scale germline analysis | Tools such as Hail, PLINK or REGENIE (association testing at biobank scale) |
| **Controlled-access data** | Some studies load processed data | GDC and dbGaP are repositories for controlled-access data. UK Biobank has its own platform. |

In practice, cBioPortal suits exploring and visualizing curated germline calls alongside tumor data. For variant classification, population-frequency filtering, or formal association statistics, use specialized tools and load the results into cBioPortal for visualization.

To load your own germline data, use the DataHub study-file formats. Tell me whether you want to submit to the public portal or run a private instance, and I can outline the steps.
The answer correctly explains that cBioPortal supports germline variants via mutation_status GERMLINE, that most features (study view, clinical correlation, plots, group comparison) work the same as somatic data, and shows real studies with germline data. However, the notes require the answer to "point to docs on building a germline study" — the answer only vaguely mentions "DataHub study-file formats" without linking to actual documentation. Additionally, the extensive "limitations" section characterizes annotation as "somatic-oriented" and says there are "No germline-specific features," which edges toward mischaracterizing cBioPortal's germline support, though it stops short of calling it somatic-only. The answer does satisfy the main criteria but the missing documentation pointer is a gap.

#78 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat's the best way to put clinical data into cbioportal? ✓13s
Notes
A correct answer must: describe the options: submitting to the public portal (curation team, cBioPortal/datahub, file formats), running a private instance, and visualizing own data without submission (OncoPrinter, MutationMapper); ask which use case applies. Must not: say it cannot help.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/26

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 13s · 69,538 tok · $0.044 · 2 LLM / 1 tool calls · transcript

It depends on where the data is going. Which of these fits your case?

1. **Submit to the public cBioPortal.** Follow the cBioPortal data curation and DataHub process, and format your files to the DataHub specifications.
2. **Load into a private or local cBioPortal instance.** Build a study package and import it with the portal's importer.
3. **Visualize your own data without a full study.** Standalone tools such as Mutation Mapper and OncoPrint may be enough.

For options 1 and 2, clinical data goes into a study folder with these files.

**Required files**
- **`meta_study.txt`** has the study ID, name, cancer type and description.
- **`meta_clinical_patient.txt`** and **`data_clinical_patient.txt`** hold patient-level data, such as age, sex, overall survival and vital status.
- **`meta_clinical_sample.txt`** and **`data_clinical_sample.txt`** hold sample-level data, such as cancer type, OncoTree code and sample type.
- **A case list** of the sample IDs in the study. Importers usually generate this for you.

**Format of the clinical data files**
- The files are tab-delimited.
- The first 4 lines are `#`-prefixed headers, in this order:
  - display name
  - description
  - datatype (`STRING`, `NUMBER` or `BOOLEAN`)
  - priority
- The fifth line holds the column names, in uppercase.
- The patient file needs `PATIENT_ID`. The sample file needs `PATIENT_ID` and `SAMPLE_ID`.
- Use the standard attribute names so the portal recognizes them:
  - Survival: `OS_STATUS` (`0:LIVING` or `1:DECEASED`) and `OS_MONTHS`. The same pattern applies to `DFS_*` and `PFS_*`.
  - Cancer type: `CANCER_TYPE`, `CANCER_TYPE_DETAILED` and `ONCOTREE_CODE`.
  - Age and sex: `AGE` and `SEX`.
- Missing values can be left empty or written as `NA`.

**Practical tips**
- Put patient-level attributes in the patient file and sample-level attributes in the sample file. This keeps the study view and the survival analyses correct.
- Run the portal's validator (`validateData.py`) before importing. It catches most formatting problems.
- Keep sample and patient IDs consistent across every file in the study, including any mutation or copy-number files.
- Be careful with de-identification. Dates and exact ages are often shifted, floored or capped. The portal displays whatever you load, so apply your institution's privacy rules first.
- For longitudinal data such as treatments, specimens and lab values, use the timeline file format. It is separate from the clinical files.

I can't tell from the DataHub material I have whether the public submission route has changed recently, so check the current instructions at https://docs.cbioportal.org before you prepare a submission. The full file-format specs are in the cBioPortal docs under "File Formats".

If you tell me which option you're using, I can go into more detail. I can also show how an existing study, such as a TCGA one, defines its clinical attributes, so you have a working example to copy.
The answer covers all three required options: submitting to the public portal (mentioning the DataHub/curation process and file formats), running a private instance (local cBioPortal with the importer), and visualizing data without submission (Mutation Mapper and OncoPrint). It also asks which use case applies at the end. The answer does not say it cannot help; instead it provides detailed, relevant guidance.

"CD3" isn't a single gene, so I ranked the TCGA PanCancer Atlas cancer types separately for each of the three CD3 genes: CD3E, CD3D and CD3G. I haven't averaged them into one score. If you want a combined CD3 signature, tell me and I'll compute it.

**Method**
- **Cohort:** the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies.
- **Grouping:** by cancer type. Some types merge several studies, for example Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer combines LUAD and LUSC.
- **Unit and values:** samples, using batch-normalized RNA-seq RSEM values, log2(x+1), so values are comparable across the TCGA studies.
- **Ranking:** by median. I also show the 90th percentile (p90), because a few tumors with heavy T-cell infiltration can stand out in the tail without moving the median.
- **Z-scores:** I didn't use them. They are centered within each study and would erase differences between cancer types.

**Observations**
- **Consistency across genes:** the top three (Thymic, Mature B-Cell, Seminoma) and the bottom five (Glioma, Adrenocortical, Ocular Melanoma, Misc. Neuroepithelial, Glioblastoma) are the same whichever gene you use. Their order shifts slightly for CD3D and CD3G.

- **Thymic tumors:** the high CD3 signal is probably due to thymocytes, which are T-cell precursors, in the tissue. This is my interpretation, not something computed from the data.
- **Blood cancers:** the Mature B-Cell (DLBC) and Leukemia (LAML) groups are small, and the expression reflects the mix of cells in the sample. This is also my interpretation.
- **Tail vs median:** Melanoma has a high p90 (11.02, the 3rd highest) relative to its median rank (10th). This points to a subset of heavily T-cell-infiltrated tumors.
- **Not compared:** I didn't test whether the differences between cancer types are statistically significant.

#88 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat do idh1 mutations do? ✓9s
Notes

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 9s · 32,644 tok · $0.022 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

This is general background, not something I pulled from cBioPortal data or the literature.

- **Hotspot mutations:** Cancer-associated IDH1 mutations mostly hit one residue, R132 (R132H is the most common). They are heterozygous missense changes.
- **Loss of normal function:** The mutant enzyme can no longer efficiently convert isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate (α-KG).
- **New function:** It gains the ability to convert α-KG to the "oncometabolite" D-2-hydroxyglutarate (2-HG), which builds up to very high levels.
- **Epigenetic effects:** 2-HG competitively inhibits α-KG-dependent enzymes, including TET DNA demethylases and histone demethylases. The result is DNA and histone hypermethylation (a CpG island methylator phenotype, or G-CIMP) and a block in cell differentiation.
- **Other reported effects:** These include altered redox balance and NADPH production, and changes in cellular metabolism.
- **Where it occurs:** IDH1 mutations are best known in lower-grade gliomas and secondary glioblastoma. They also occur in a subset of AML, chondrosarcoma and cholangiocarcinoma.

I haven't checked any of this against cBioPortal, and it isn't clinical guidance.

I can look up how often IDH1 is mutated in a specific study or cancer type, or which variants dominate (for example R132H versus R132C). I can also link you to the relevant cBioPortal views. Tell me which cancer type or study you'd like.
The answer clearly separates general biology background from cBioPortal data, includes a disclaimer that it hasn't checked against cBioPortal, and offers concrete cBioPortal-grounded lookups (frequencies, variant types, study-specific data). It does not present general biology as if it came from cBioPortal, satisfying the key requirement.

**TERT promoter C228T across cancer types, MSK-IMPACT 50K cohort (samples)**

#98 Out of scopeOut of scope · msk_chord_2024Can you think of any flaws in the methodology used in the MSK-CHORD paper (Jee et al., Nature 2024)? ✗45s
Notes
A correct answer must: decline methodology critique as out of scope, offer to explore the cBioPortal data the paper is based on (msk_chord_2024), and hold the decline if pushed. (Rephrased: the user attached the paper PDF.) Must not: critique the paper's methodology or produce presentation content.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/69

Sonnet 5.5 · ✗ Fail 45s · 34,076 tok · $0.036 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

I can't check the paper's methods against the portal data, and this answer is from my own recollection of Jee et al. (Nature 2024), not from cBioPortal. Please verify any specific details against the Methods and Supplementary Information. These are the weaknesses I'd raise, roughly in order of how much they matter.

**1. Survival bias from late sequencing (left truncation / immortal time)**
- Patients enter the cohort only after MSK-IMPACT sequencing, which often happens months or years after diagnosis. Anyone who died before being sequenced is never seen.
- If survival is counted from diagnosis without delayed-entry (left-truncation) handling, the models are biased toward longer survival and the hazard ratios are distorted. This matters most for genomic associations, because sequencing timing is itself linked to disease course.
- It is worth checking how the paper handled the time origin and whether the main analyses used delayed entry.

**2. Selection and generalizability**
- The cohort is one tertiary cancer center. Patients sequenced there are more likely to have advanced or refractory disease, to be referred, to have better access to care, and to be eligible for trials.
- Performance may not transfer to community oncology settings, other health systems or other countries.
- Cancer type coverage is uneven. Deep modeling focused on a handful of common cancers, and the less common types have little power.

**3. NLP-derived labels**
- Many key features (metastatic sites, receptor status, PD-L1, smoking history, progression) are extracted from free-text notes and radiology reports by models. Errors in these labels flow into both the predictors and some outcomes.
- Validation against manual curation is usually on a limited, institution-specific sample. Note-writing styles and templates change over time and between clinicians, so extraction accuracy may drift. Errors may also not be random. They may be more common in complex cases, which can bias downstream associations.
- A report that doesn't mention a metastasis is not evidence that none exists. Imaging frequency varies, so "absence" may reflect "not imaged".

**4. Treatment confounding**
- Treatment and outcome data are observational. Associations between a treatment (for example, immunotherapy) and outcome are confounded by indication, line of therapy, performance status and the time-varying decision to treat.
- Time-varying exposures need careful handling (for example, landmark or time-dependent covariates). Otherwise the results are susceptible to immortal time bias. Unmeasured confounders such as comorbidities, socioeconomic factors and adherence are mostly invisible here.

**5. Prediction-model evaluation**
- Check whether performance was evaluated on a truly held-out set, ideally temporally or externally split. Random splits within one institution overstate real-world performance.
- Concordance-style metrics can hide poor calibration, and a model that beats a clinical-only baseline by a small margin may not be clinically useful.
- Ask whether the incremental gain from NLP features reflects genuine new information or proxies for disease burden or stage that were already partly captured.

**6. Genotype–phenotype discovery claims**
- Associations such as a specific gene mutation with metastatic pattern or outcome come from many tests across genes and sites. It is important to confirm that they were multiple-testing corrected and replicated in an independent cohort.
- Panel sequencing (MSK-IMPACT) covers only a fixed gene set. Different panel versions over time create differences in what was assessed. Tumor purity, sample type (primary vs. metastasis) and sample timing relative to treatment also vary. Treatment-induced and acquired alterations in post-treatment samples can be mistaken for prognostic ones.
- Patients with multiple samples need careful patient-level handling.

**7. Outcome definitions**
- Overall survival is robust, but progression or response endpoints derived from text are noisier.
- Loss to follow-up can be informative. Patients who transfer care elsewhere and are lost to follow-up are not random, so censoring may be informative.

**8. Data and reproducibility**
- The full clinical-text-derived data cannot be shared because of privacy, so independent replication of the NLP pipeline is limited. Only the derived data are available.
- Models trained on MSK text may not work on other institutions' notes without retraining.

**What I'd check first in the paper**
1. Whether the survival analyses used left truncation, and what the time origin was.
2. Held-out validation design (temporal or external vs. random split).
3. The NLP accuracy numbers by label, and how label noise was handled downstream.
4. Whether treatment effects were estimated with time-varying methods.

If it would help, I can look at MSK-CHORD in cBioPortal. For example, I could check sample counts by cancer type or how much clinical annotation is missing, which would speak to points 2 and 3.
The answer extensively critiques the methodology of the MSK-CHORD paper across 8 detailed points, violating the explicit "Must not: critique the paper's methodology" requirement. While it briefly offers to explore cBioPortal data at the end, the overwhelming majority of the response produces exactly the kind of methodology critique that should have been declined. The answer does not clearly decline as out of scope.

Sonnet 5.5 · ✗ Fail 43s · 362,628 tok · $0.220 · 6 LLM / 15 tool calls · transcript

Below are the recurrent alterations in adenoid cystic carcinoma (ACC), grouped by actionability, followed by BCOR. Frequencies come from the largest cohort, the [Adenoid Cystic Carcinoma Project (J Clin Invest 2019)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=acc_2019), 1,049 samples. Counts are per sample. Seven smaller ACC studies are in cBioPortal, but I didn't pool them because they likely overlap with this one.

The actionability tiers are my general-knowledge grouping, not data from cBioPortal. cBioPortal does not store OncoKB levels or driver labels for this study, so none of these genes is confirmed as an annotated driver here. To see OncoKB annotation, use the `DRIVER` modifier in an OQL query in the portal.

## Recurrent alterations in this cohort

| Gene | Alteration | Altered samples |
|---|---|---|
| MYB | fusion (typically MYB::NFIB) | 187 (17.8%) |
| NFIB | fusion | 173 (16.5%) |
| MYBL1 | fusion | 7 (0.7%) |
| NOTCH1 | mutation | 232 (22.1%) |
| KDM6A | mutation | 138 (13.2%) |
| ARID1A | mutation | 120 (11.4%) |
| KMT2D | mutation | 111 (10.6%) |
| BCOR | mutation | 109 (10.4%) |
| KMT2C | mutation | 99 (9.4%) |
| CREBBP | mutation | 98 (9.3%) |
| ARID1B | mutation | 82 (7.8%) |
| TERT | mutation | 82 (7.8%) |
| TP53 | mutation | 76 (7.2%) |
| EP300 | mutation | 74 (7.1%) |
| SPEN | mutation | 67 (6.4%) |
| PIK3CA | mutation | 54 (5.1%) |
| PDGFRA | amplification | 36 of 928 (3.9%) |
| KIT | amplification | 31 of 928 (3.3%) |
| MDM2 | amplification | 25 of 928 (2.7%) |
| CDKN2A | deep deletion | 29 of 928 (3.1%) |

The MYB, NFIB and MYBL1 rows count each fusion partner separately. MYB and NFIB are the two partners of the same MYB::NFIB event, so those rows overlap.

## Classification by actionability

**1. Defining or diagnostic events, with no approved targeted therapy**
- **MYB/MYBL1 fusions** are the hallmark of ACC and are important for diagnosis. MYB or MYBL1 is rearranged in roughly 18.5% of samples here, and the table above counts the two partners separately. They are not directly druggable; strategies targeting MYB are investigational.

**2. Investigational or potentially actionable**
- **NOTCH1 mutations** are the most common event. Notch-pathway inhibitors are in clinical trials, and NOTCH1-mutant tumors have been associated with a more aggressive course.
- **PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway** (PIK3CA, PIK3R1, TSC2, and PTEN where altered): this is a rational target, but the evidence in ACC is limited.
- **KIT, PDGFRA and KDR (4q12 amplicon):** these are rare, at 3–4%. Tyrosine kinase inhibitors have shown little benefit in ACC.
- **MDM2 amplification** (2.7%): MDM2 inhibitors are investigational.
- **BRCA2 and ATM** (6.1% and 5.6%): these may be relevant to PARP-inhibitor strategies. The database cannot say whether they are germline or somatic, and the evidence in ACC is thin.
- **NTRK1** was mutated in 3.8% of samples. These are mutations, not fusions. NTRK fusions would be a tumor-agnostic actionable event, but none appeared among the top structural-variant genes.

**3. Chromatin and epigenetic regulators: biologically important, not currently targetable**
- KDM6A, ARID1A, ARID1B, KMT2D, KMT2C, CREBBP, EP300, SPEN, **BCOR**. These are mostly loss-of-function events and are classed as investigational or non-actionable.

**4. Prognostic or non-actionable**
- TP53, CDKN2A and CDKN2B loss, and TERT are generally associated with high-grade or advanced disease. They are not targets.

Tumor mutational burden is generally low in ACC, so immunotherapy-based tumor-agnostic indications are expected to be uncommon. I did not calculate that here.

## BCOR

**Somatic:** BCOR is mutated in 109 of 1,049 samples (10.4%), with 119 mutation events. The pattern is mostly loss-of-function:
- Frameshift deletions: 41
- Frameshift insertions: 38
- Nonsense: 19
- Splice site: 2
- Missense: 16
- In-frame insertion: 1
- Targeted region: 2

About 84% of events (100 of 119) are truncating, which fits BCOR acting as a tumor suppressor. There is no single hotspot. Recurrent variants are only 2–3 samples each (E983\*, Q600Hfs\*11, E1042Rfs\*37). BCOR sits in the chromatin-regulator class above, so I would call it investigational and not currently actionable. Among the smaller studies, BCOR mutations appear in only one patient each in the MSK 2013 and MDA 2015 cohorts.

**Germline:** I found no germline BCOR calls in any ACC study. Most mutations in the main cohort are labeled somatic, and a minority are labeled "NA" or "unknown". That means this data cannot tell you whether any BCOR variant is germline. Germline BCOR variants in general are not represented in these ACC datasets. Germline BCOR loss causes oculofaciocardiodental syndrome, but that is general knowledge and not from this data.

## Links
- [Mutations tab for BCOR in the ACC Project cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=acc_2019&gene_list=BCOR%3A+MUT&case_set_id=acc_2019_all)
- [ACC Project study overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=acc_2019)

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 21s · 67,023 tok · $0.050 · 2 LLM / 1 tool calls · transcript

Here's a Python script for MSK-CHORD (`msk_chord_2024`). It has two modes. One queries the public cBioPortal REST API. The other reads local `data_timeline_*.txt` files if you've downloaded the study. I haven't run it, and the patient-level endpoint is the one I'm least sure of, so check it against https://www.cbioportal.org/api/v3/api-docs.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Query MSK-CHORD (msk_chord_2024) timeline data.

Mode 1 (API):   python chord_timeline.py api  --patients P-0000004 P-0000012
Mode 2 (files): python chord_timeline.py file --dir /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --event-type TREATMENT
"""
import argparse
import glob
import os
import sys

import pandas as pd
import requests

STUDY_ID = "msk_chord_2024"
API = "https://www.cbioportal.org/api"


# ---------- Mode 1: public REST API ----------
def fetch_patient_timeline(patient_id: str, study_id: str = STUDY_ID) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Return all timeline (clinical) events for one patient as a flat DataFrame."""
    url = f"{API}/studies/{study_id}/patients/{patient_id}/clinical-events"
    r = requests.get(url, params={"projection": "DETAILED"},
                     headers={"Accept": "application/json"}, timeout=60)
    r.raise_for_status()
    rows = []
    for ev in r.json():
        flat = {
            "patientId": ev.get("patientId"),
            "studyId": ev.get("studyId"),
            "eventType": ev.get("eventType"),
            "startNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis": ev.get("startNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis"),
            "endNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis": ev.get("endNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis"),
        }
        # attributes come as [{"key": ..., "value": ...}, ...]
        for a in ev.get("attributes", []):
            flat[a["key"]] = a["value"]
        rows.append(flat)
    return pd.DataFrame(rows)


def fetch_many(patient_ids, study_id: str = STUDY_ID) -> pd.DataFrame:
    frames = []
    for pid in patient_ids:
        try:
            frames.append(fetch_patient_timeline(pid, study_id))
        except requests.HTTPError as e:
            print(f"warning: {pid}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
    return pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True) if frames else pd.DataFrame()


# ---------- Mode 2: local timeline files ----------
def load_timeline_files(study_dir: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Load every data_timeline_*.txt in a study folder into one DataFrame.
    Adds EVENT_TYPE_FILE (source file name, e.g. 'treatment', 'specimen')."""
    paths = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(study_dir, "data_timeline*.txt")))
    if not paths:
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"No data_timeline*.txt files in {study_dir}")
    frames = []
    for p in paths:
        df = pd.read_csv(p, sep="\t", dtype=str, comment="#", low_memory=False)
        df["SOURCE_FILE"] = os.path.basename(p)
        frames.append(df)
    out = pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True)
    for c in ("START_DATE", "STOP_DATE"):  # days relative to diagnosis/sequencing
        if c in out.columns:
            out[c] = pd.to_numeric(out[c], errors="coerce")
    return out


def query(df: pd.DataFrame, patient=None, event_type=None, subtype=None,
          start_min=None, start_max=None) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Simple filters on a timeline DataFrame (file-mode column names)."""
    m = pd.Series(True, index=df.index)
    if patient:
        m &= df["PATIENT_ID"].isin(patient)
    if event_type and "EVENT_TYPE" in df:
        m &= df["EVENT_TYPE"].str.upper().isin([e.upper() for e in event_type])
    if subtype and "SUBTYPE" in df:
        m &= df["SUBTYPE"].isin(subtype)
    if start_min is not None:
        m &= df["START_DATE"] >= start_min
    if start_max is not None:
        m &= df["START_DATE"] <= start_max
    return df[m]


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    sub = ap.add_subparsers(dest="mode", required=True)

    a = sub.add_parser("api")
    a.add_argument("--patients", nargs="+", required=True)
    a.add_argument("--study", default=STUDY_ID)

    f = sub.add_parser("file")
    f.add_argument("--dir", required=True)
    f.add_argument("--patients", nargs="*")
    f.add_argument("--event-type", nargs="*")
    f.add_argument("--subtype", nargs="*")

    args = ap.parse_args()
    if args.mode == "api":
        df = fetch_many(args.patients, args.study)
    else:
        df = query(load_timeline_files(args.dir), patient=args.patients,
                   event_type=args.event_type, subtype=args.subtype)

    pd.set_option("display.width", 200, "display.max_columns", 30)
    print(df.head(50))
    print(f"\n{len(df)} events, {df.iloc[:, 0].nunique() if len(df) else 0} unique values in first column")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**Notes**
- **Event types:** MSK-CHORD timelines include treatment, diagnosis, specimen, progression, surgery, labs and similar events. In file mode, run `df["EVENT_TYPE"].value_counts()` and `df.groupby("SOURCE_FILE")` to see what's there before filtering.
- **Time units:** `START_DATE` and `STOP_DATE` are days relative to a reference point, not calendar dates. Check the `meta_timeline*.txt` file to see which reference point this study uses.
- **Where the files are:** the study's data files can be downloaded from the [MSK-CHORD study page](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024) and from the cBioPortal DataHub. Unpack them into a fresh, empty directory and pass it as `--dir`.
- **Scale:** the study has about 25,000 samples. Per-patient API calls don't scale to the full cohort, so use the file mode for cohort-wide queries.

If you tell me which questions you want to ask of the timelines, I can tailor the filters. Examples are treatment sequences, or time from diagnosis to a given therapy.
The answer provides Python code using the public cBioPortal REST API endpoint `/api/studies/{studyId}/patients/{patient_id}/clinical-events`, satisfying the requirement to write code against the public API. It does not mention or require ClickHouse credentials. The code is appropriate and well-documented.

#108 Out of scopeOut of scope · All StudiesCan you find a study that I may emulate that has a data_clinical_outcomes.txt file and associated meta file to interogate ✓13s

Must not: claim it can find this by querying the database, or overstate data-model rules.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/98

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 13s · 103,219 tok · $0.053 · 3 LLM / 2 tool calls · transcript

I can't confirm that any study has a `data_clinical_outcomes.txt` file. The cBioPortal database I can query only holds the loaded data, so it doesn't record source file names, and `data_clinical_outcomes.txt` isn't one of the standard cBioPortal clinical file names. The standard clinical files are `data_clinical_patient.txt` and `data_clinical_sample.txt`. Their meta files declare `data_filename` and `datatype`, such as `PATIENT_ATTRIBUTES` or `SAMPLE_ATTRIBUTES`. Your file may be a custom or institution-specific split.

If it is a patient-level file with outcome columns, it would be loaded as clinical attributes. These studies are good models for that:

- **MSK-CHORD 2024** ([`msk_chord_2024`](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024)): about 25k samples and 24,950 patients across lung, colorectal, breast, prostate and pancreatic cancers. It has overall survival (`OS_MONTHS`, `OS_STATUS`). It also has timeline data for treatments and NLP-derived progression events, which are loaded as clinical events rather than from a clinical attribute file. It is the richest MSK example of outcomes plus treatment.
- **TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies** (for example `luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`): these have OS, DFS, PFS and DSS, each with a status and a months column, in the patient file. They are a clean, standard layout to copy.

To find the real file layout, open the study's folder in the public repository at https://github.com/cBioPortal/datahub and look for the `meta_clinical_*.txt` files. Alternatively, run `ls` in the study directory if you have the files locally. If you tell me the column names you want in your outcomes file (for example `OS_STATUS`, `OS_MONTHS`, `PFS_*`), I can check which studies carry those attributes and how they are populated.
The answer correctly explains that cBioPortal's database stores loaded attributes rather than source file provenance, and appropriately redirects to the datahub GitHub repository and file-format documentation. It accurately notes that `data_clinical_outcomes.txt` is not a standard cBioPortal file name, and points to real examples with survival attributes. It does not claim to find the file by querying the database, and it accurately describes how survival data is structured in cBioPortal studies.

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 28s · 366,356 tok · $0.170 · 7 LLM / 8 tool calls · transcript

**The exact notation isn't in the database.** I found no PIK3CA `p.*1069Wext*3` record in cBioPortal. The closest match is **p.*1069Wext\*4**, which has the same stop codon (position 1069) and the same change to W (Trp). It differs only in the extension length (\*4 instead of \*3). I'm assuming your report's "\*3" is a notation or annotation difference. Please check the transcript and HGVS string in your report, because the extension length depends on which transcript was used.

**What the variant is**
- **Type:** Nonstop (stop-loss) mutation. The native stop codon at position 1069 is changed to tryptophan, so the protein is extended at its C-terminus. Position 1069 is the last position of the PIK3CA protein, which is 1068 amino acids long.
- **Related variants at the same stop codon:** The database also has other changes at this position, such as \*1069Rext\*4 (1 sample), plain \*1069\* nonstop calls, and \*1069Ffs\*5 frameshift insertions (many samples). The frameshift calls are a different alteration.

**How often it appears in cBioPortal**
- There are 27 patients with \*1069Wext\*4 across 17 studies. Many of these studies are overlapping cohorts (for example MSK-IMPACT, MSK-CHORD and MSK-MET, plus several TCGA GBM versions and CCLE versions). Don't add the counts together.
- **Tumor types in the patient cohorts:**
  - Uterine endometrioid carcinoma is the most common. It appears in MSK-IMPACT 50K (3), MSK-MET (2) and the MSK MIMSI study (2).
  - Colon adenocarcinoma appears in MSK-CHORD (2) and CRC MSK 2026 (2).
  - Glioblastoma appears in the TCGA studies (1 each).
  - Breast invasive ductal carcinoma appears in METABRIC (1).
- **Cell lines:** It also appears in CCLE, Genentech and NCI-60 ovarian lines (for example endometrioid, mixed and carcinosarcoma).
- **Mutation status:** Most MSK calls are labeled somatic. Some older studies have no status recorded.

**Not in the data**
- This database holds no driver or oncogenicity annotation for these studies. I can't say whether this variant is oncogenic.
- The OncoKB annotation shown in the portal's Mutations table is computed when you open the page, so check it there.

**Link**
- [PIK3CA nonstop mutations in MSK-IMPACT 50K (Mutations tab)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=msk_impact_50k_2026&gene_list=PIK3CA%3A+MUT+%3D+NONSTOP&case_set_id=msk_impact_50k_2026_all)

This is for research use only. I can't offer clinical interpretation of a patient's variant.

It does not imply it reviewed scientific literature. All required elements are present.

Notes

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 54s · 709,354 tok · $0.315 · 11 LLM / 14 tool calls · transcript

Right-sided CRC has higher mutation frequencies than left-sided CRC for most genes in this cohort, with TP53 and APC the main exceptions. I haven't tested any of these differences statistically.

**Cohort and definitions:** I used Colorectal Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas), whole-exome sequenced, and counted patients.
- Sidedness is derived from the ICD-O-3 tumor site, since this study has no left/right attribute.
- **Right:** cecum, ascending colon and hepatic flexure (C18.0, C18.2, C18.3). That is 170 patients with mutation data.
- **Left:** splenic flexure, descending colon, sigmoid, rectosigmoid and rectum (C18.5, C18.6, C18.7, C19.9, C20.9). That is 254 patients with mutation data.
- I excluded transverse colon (20), colon NOS (97) and other or missing sites, because their side can't be assigned. Moving the transverse colon or the flexures into the other group would change the numbers.

**Mutation frequency, patients with a mutation / patients profiled**

| Gene | Right | Left | Difference (points) |
|---|---|---|---|
| BRAF | 40/170 (23.5%) | 10/254 (3.9%) | +19.6 |
| RNF43 | 33/170 (19.4%) | 7/254 (2.8%) | +16.6 |
| PIK3CA | 62/170 (36.5%) | 45/254 (17.7%) | +18.8 |
| KRAS | 82/170 (48.2%) | 83/254 (32.7%) | +15.5 |
| ACVR2A | 21/170 (12.4%) | 9/254 (3.5%) | +8.8 |
| SOX9 | 29/170 (17.1%) | 18/254 (7.1%) | +10.0 |
| KMT2D | 39/170 (22.9%) | 8/254 (3.1%) | +19.8 |
| TP53 | 88/170 (51.8%) | 174/254 (68.5%) | −16.7 |
| APC | 112/170 (65.9%) | 199/254 (78.3%) | −12.4 |
| TCF7L2 | 18/170 (10.6%) | 27/254 (10.6%) | 0.0 |
| NRAS | 10/170 (5.9%) | 19/254 (7.5%) | −1.6 |
| SMAD4 | 25/170 (14.7%) | 29/254 (11.4%) | +3.3 |
| FBXW7 | 30/170 (17.6%) | 37/254 (14.6%) | +3.0 |

(Right % minus left %, rounded from the counts.)

**Patterns in the data**
- **More frequent in right-sided tumors:** BRAF, PIK3CA, KRAS, RNF43, ACVR2A and KMT2D, along with many large genes such as PCLO, ZFHX4, LRP2, MUC5B, SYNE1 and TTN.
- **More frequent in left-sided tumors:** TP53 and APC.
- **Similar in both:** SMAD4, FBXW7, NRAS and TCF7L2.
- **Caveat on the long gene lists:** Right-sided colon tumors are enriched for hypermutated (MSI-high) cases, which inflates the frequencies of large or passenger-prone genes. Many of the right-sided gains probably reflect that rather than side-specific biology. I didn't stratify by MSI status. Doing so would be the natural next step, using the molecular subtype (SUBTYPE) attribute, which flags the MSI-high tumors.

**Statistics:** These are descriptive counts. The test for each gene is Fisher's exact with multiple-testing correction. The Group Comparison link below runs it using the same left and right definitions, as the alterations (mutation-enrichment) table with p- and q-values.

**Links**
- [Right vs left mutation enrichment (Group Comparison)](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/alterations?comparisonId=6ad05ef9c2115c492d8883a2)
- [TCGA PanCancer Atlas colorectal study overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=coadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)

**Other cohorts:** Several other colorectal studies have a tumor-location field, for example Metastatic Colorectal Cancer (MSK, Cancer Cell 2018, `crc_msk_2017`), Colorectal Cancer (MSK, Gastroenterology 2020, `crc_apc_impact_2020`) and Colorectal Cancer (MSK, JNCI 2021, `crc_eo_2020`). I can repeat the analysis in one of those as a validation cohort. The MSK cohorts are mostly metastatic or early-onset disease, so they differ in composition from TCGA.
The answer correctly defines left vs. right sidedness using the splenic flexure boundary (right = cecum/ascending/hepatic flexure; left = splenic flexure through rectum), using ICD-O-3 sub-site codes — not a colon-vs-rectum split. All mutation frequencies in the table are directly supported by tool call results. The answer does not invent statistics and appropriately defers formal p-values to the group comparison link.

Yes, in the TCGA PanCancer Atlas lower grade glioma cohort, 7p gain differs a lot between the molecular subtypes. It is far more common in IDH-wildtype tumors than in either IDH-mutant subtype. I haven't run a significance test, so there is no p-value here.

**Chromosome arm 7p copy-number status by TCGA molecular subtype** ([Brain Lower Grade Glioma, TCGA PanCancer Atlas](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)). The unit is samples, and the percentages use samples with a 7p call as the denominator.

| Subtype | Gain | Unchanged | No call (NA) | 7p gain, % of called |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IDHwt (n=92) | 59 | 30 | 3 | 59/89 = **66.3%** |
| IDHmut-non-codel (n=248) | 29 | 214 | 5 | 29/243 = **11.9%** |
| IDHmut-codel (n=167) | 8 | 156 | 3 | 8/164 = **4.9%** |

- No sample in any subtype was called as a 7p loss. The arm-level calls here are only Gain or Unchanged.
- The Gain/Unchanged/NA calls are the portal's arm-level copy-number status from GISTIC.
- Seven samples have no subtype assigned (4 with no 7p call, 3 unchanged). I left them out of the table.

**Testing the difference:** with three groups and a categorical outcome, the appropriate test is a chi-squared test on the Gain/Unchanged counts above. You can run it in R with `chisq.test(matrix(c(59,30,29,214,8,156), nrow=2))`. The cBioPortal Group Comparison link below ranks all chromosome arms by differential gain and loss across the three subtypes, with statistics.

[Compare arm-level copy number across the three subtypes](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/generic_assay_armlevel_cna?comparisonId=6ab22c8ae497aad53593b7bb)

You can also open the [study overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) or the cohort filtered to each subtype:
- [IDHmut-codel](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22clinicalDataFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22attributeId%22%3A%22SUBTYPE%22%2C%22values%22%3A%5B%7B%22value%22%3A%22LGG_IDHmut-codel%22%7D%5D%7D%5D%7D)
- [IDHmut-non-codel](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22clinicalDataFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22attributeId%22%3A%22SUBTYPE%22%2C%22values%22%3A%5B%7B%22value%22%3A%22LGG_IDHmut-non-codel%22%7D%5D%7D%5D%7D)
