Yes. In TCGA lower grade glioma, the descriptive numbers show large methylation differences between the molecular subtypes, mainly IDH-wildtype versus IDH-mutant. I did not run any statistical tests. The values below are descriptive, so I can't give p-values or call any difference significant.

**Cohort:** TCGA lower grade glioma (HM450 methylation array, gene-level beta values). Subtype labels come from the PanCancer Atlas version of the same patients. I joined the two by patient ID, which gave 521 patients with both methylation and subtype data.

**Genome-wide average methylation** (each sample's average beta value across genes, then summarized per subtype):

| Subtype | Patients | Median (IQR) |
|---|---|---|
| IDH-wildtype | 94 | 0.384 (0.372–0.397) |
| IDH-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted | 170 | 0.462 (0.448–0.478) |
| IDH-mutant, non-codeleted | 257 | 0.454 (0.441–0.466) |

- The IDH-wildtype tumors have lower average methylation, and their interquartile range does not overlap those of the two IDH-mutant groups.
- The two IDH-mutant groups are close to each other. The codeleted group is slightly higher.
- This fits the IDH-mutant tumors having a hypermethylated phenotype. That reading is general knowledge, not something I derived from this data.

**Gene-level examples** (median beta by subtype):

| Gene | IDH-wildtype | Codeleted | Non-codeleted |
|---|---|---|---|
| MGMT | 0.03 | 0.55 | 0.40 |
| TERT | 0.76 | 0.86 | 0.77 |
| CDKN2A | 0.18 | 0.15 | 0.14 |

- MGMT is almost unmethylated in IDH-wildtype tumors and is stepwise higher in the non-codeleted and codeleted groups.
- TERT is higher in the codeleted group.
- CDKN2A is low in all three groups, so it doesn't separate them.

**Genes with the largest subtype gaps** (mean beta, wildtype / codeleted / non-codeleted):
- PLIN5: 0.05 / 0.84 / 0.67
- RASGRP2: 0.08 / 0.83 / 0.83
- MRC2: 0.05 / 0.80 / 0.76
- TLE4: 0.05 / 0.79 / 0.75

I picked these by the size of the gap, not by a test. Most of the largest gaps separate IDH-wildtype from both IDH-mutant groups. PLIN5 and SYPL1 also show a clear step between the two IDH-mutant groups.

**Explore and test it in cBioPortal:**
- [Methylation group comparison (3 subtypes)](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/generic_assay_methylation?comparisonId=6ad06030e497aad53593f74a). It compares IDH-wildtype (92 samples), IDH-mutant codeleted (167) and IDH-mutant non-codeleted (248), and ranks probes by differential methylation with statistics.
- [Study view of the PanCancer Atlas cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)

Sonnet 5.5 · – Declined 11s · 87,209 tok · $0.084 · 2 LLM / 3 tool calls · transcript

"Point mutation" can mean different things, and "lung cancer" covers several studies. Which do you mean?

**1. What counts as a point mutation?**
- **Missense only**: single amino-acid substitutions, such as L858R.
- **All single-nucleotide variants**: missense, nonsense and splice-site changes. Synonymous (silent) variants are left out of most cBioPortal studies, so they would add little.
- **All EGFR mutations**: this also adds in-frame indels such as exon 19 deletions, and frameshifts. These are the main EGFR alterations in lung cancer, and a point-mutation-only view would miss them.

**2. Which lung cancer cohort?** I can use any of these:
- **TCGA PanCancer Atlas** lung adenocarcinoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma, reported separately.
- **MSK-IMPACT 50k**, filtered to lung subtypes. It is much larger and better suited to EGFR, since it is a targeted panel with many lung patients.
- A specific study that you name.

I'd suggest missense plus the other EGFR variants in MSK-IMPACT 50k, with a per-variant breakdown (L858R, T790M, exon 19 deletions and so on). Tell me which option you want, or say "your call" and I'll go with that.

I used the TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohorts and compared mutation frequency in KRAS-mutant vs KRAS-wild-type samples. Counts are samples, and the denominators are samples profiled for each gene. Only mutations are counted, not copy number or fusions.

## Colorectal adenocarcinoma (TCGA PanCancer Atlas)
KRAS-mutant: 218 samples. KRAS wild-type: 316 samples.

| Gene | KRAS-mutant | KRAS wild-type |
|---|---|---|
| APC | 182/218 (83.5%) | 205/316 (64.9%) |
| PIK3CA | 83/218 (38.1%) | 64/316 (20.3%) |
| BRAF | 6/218 (2.8%) | 56/316 (17.7%) |
| NRAS | 5/218 (2.3%) | 28/316 (8.9%) |
| ARID1A | 15/218 (6.9%) | 43/316 (13.6%) |

- APC and PIK3CA mutations are more frequent in the KRAS-mutant group.
- BRAF and NRAS mutations are less frequent in the KRAS-mutant group.
- The remaining top-ranked genes, such as COL7A1, ABCA13, DOCK3 and ZNF469, are mostly large genes. Their mutations are probably passengers in hypermutated tumors rather than real KRAS associations.

## Lung adenocarcinoma (TCGA PanCancer Atlas)
KRAS-mutant: 168 samples. KRAS wild-type: 398 samples.

| Gene | KRAS-mutant | KRAS wild-type |
|---|---|---|
| STK11 | 38/168 (22.6%) | 37/398 (9.3%) |
| ATM | 28/168 (16.7%) | 19/398 (4.8%) |
| TP53 | 62/168 (36.9%) | 233/398 (58.5%) |
| EGFR | 1/168 (0.6%) | 69/398 (17.3%) |
| NF1 | 8/168 (4.8%) | 58/398 (14.6%) |
| BRAF | 3/168 (1.8%) | 38/398 (9.5%) |

- STK11 and ATM mutations are more frequent in the KRAS-mutant group.
- TP53, EGFR, NF1 and BRAF mutations are less frequent in the KRAS-mutant group.
- MUC16, CSMD1 and LRP1B are also higher in KRAS-mutant samples, but these are large genes that are often mutated by chance.

These are descriptive frequency differences only. I haven't computed any significance tests. The Alterations tab in the links below runs Fisher's exact test, with multiple-testing correction, across all genes.

- [CRC: KRAS-mutant vs unaltered, alterations comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/alterations?cancer_study_list=coadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%3A+MUT&case_set_id=coadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&profileFilter=mutations)
- [LUAD: KRAS-mutant vs unaltered, alterations comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/alterations?cancer_study_list=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%3A+MUT&case_set_id=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&profileFilter=mutations)

**EGFR mutation frequency, lung vs. brain (TCGA PanCancer Atlas, sample-level)**

I used lung = LUAD + LUSC and brain = GBM + LGG. Frequency is mutated samples divided by samples profiled for EGFR.

| Cohort | EGFR-mutated | Profiled | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lung adenocarcinoma ([LUAD](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)) | 70 | 566 | 12.4% |
| Lung squamous ([LUSC](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)) | 14 | 484 | 2.9% |
| **Lung combined** | 84 | 1,050 | 8.0% |
| Glioblastoma ([GBM](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)) | 94 | 397 | 23.7% |
| Lower-grade glioma ([LGG](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)) | 35 | 514 | 6.8% |
| **Brain combined** | 129 | 911 | 14.2% |

The combined rows are simple sums of the per-study counts. The TCGA studies are non-overlapping.

**Recurrent variants** (variants in at least 4 samples):
- **LUAD:** L858R in 23 samples (4.1%) and the exon 19 deletion E746_A750del in 16 (2.8%). Both are kinase-domain alterations.
- **GBM:** extracellular-domain missense variants. A289V is in 16 samples (4.0%), G598V in 15 (3.8%), A289T in 6, R222C in 6, A289D in 5, and R108K in 4. V774M, in 5 samples (1.3%), is the one recurrent variant outside the extracellular domain.
- **LGG:** only A289V (6 samples) and G598V (6 samples) recur.
- **LUSC:** no variant reaches 4 samples.

**Summary:** Brain tumors have the higher overall EGFR mutation rate, driven mainly by GBM. The variant spectrum differs between the two organs. Lung adenocarcinoma carries the classic kinase-domain hotspots, while glioma variants cluster in the extracellular domain, at A289 and G598.

I didn't compute a significance test. The comparison is a 2×2 table of mutated vs. not mutated, so Fisher's exact test applies. The Group Comparison link below runs it for you, with both a per-gene test and a per-protein-change breakdown.

[Open Lung vs. Brain group comparison (Mutations tab, EGFR)](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/mutations?comparisonId=6ad05540c2115c492d88836d&selectedGene=EGFR)

Groups in that comparison: lung 1,053 samples and brain 1,106 samples. These are total study samples, not the EGFR-profiled counts in the table above.

These counts are all non-synonymous mutations of any kind. I didn't check driver annotation. Copy-number amplification of EGFR is common in GBM, but it isn't included here.

- **Chondrosarcoma:** conventional chondrosarcoma only. Dedifferentiated, myxoid, extraskeletal myxoid and mesenchymal subtypes are not included.

#141 AnalysisExpression & multi-omics · All Studiesis there a relatinoship between mgmt methylation and idh1 mutation in glioma? ✓34s

Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-navigator/issues/46

Sonnet 5.5 · ✓ Pass 34s · 320,260 tok · $0.158 · 7 LLM / 11 tool calls · transcript

Yes, the data suggest a relationship. IDH1-mutant gliomas have MGMT promoter methylation more often than IDH1-wild-type gliomas. The effect is much stronger in the TCGA cohort than in the MSK cohort. I haven't run a significance test, so these are counts only.

Counts are patients with a reported MGMT status. IDH1 status is from somatic mutation calls.

**Merged LGG and GBM cohort (TCGA, Cell 2016, `lgggbm_tcga_pub`), n = 932**

| | MGMT methylated | MGMT unmethylated | % methylated |
|---|---|---|---|
| IDH1-mutant | 379 | 31 | 92.4% |
| IDH1-wild-type | 234 | 288 | 44.8% |

**Glioma (MSK, Clin Cancer Res 2019, `glioma_mskcc_2019`), n = 636**

| | MGMT methylated | MGMT unmethylated | % methylated |
|---|---|---|---|
| IDH1-mutant | 101 | 79 | 56.1% |
| IDH1-wild-type | 140 | 316 | 30.7% |

**Another cohort that looks the same:** GLASS 2019 (`difg_glass_2019`), n = 105. The 36 IDH1-mutant patients split 33 methylated and 3 unmethylated. The 69 IDH1-wild-type patients split 29 methylated and 40 unmethylated.

**Caveats**
- **Mixed grades:** Both main cohorts mix lower-grade glioma and glioblastoma. IDH status tracks closely with grade and histology, so some of the association may come from that mix. You could check it by stratifying on grade or histology.
- **Different MGMT assays:** The cohorts used different methods to call MGMT status, so the percentages aren't directly comparable between them.
- **Study selection:** I used the cohorts with an explicit MGMT status field and left out two small ones. UCSF 2014 and MSK 2023 are almost entirely IDH1-mutant, so they can't show a contrast.
- **Panel coverage:** I counted every patient with an MGMT status as profiled for IDH1. If a study used a targeted panel, a patient with no IDH1 mutation may not have been fully covered.

**Significance test:** These are 2×2 tables, so Fisher's exact test (two-tailed) is the right test. You can get it from the Group Comparison page below, which compares IDH1-altered and unaltered samples on MGMT status. In R, use `fisher.test(matrix(c(379,31,234,288), nrow=2))` for TCGA and `fisher.test(matrix(c(101,79,140,316), nrow=2))` for MSK.

**Links**
- [IDH1-mutant vs unaltered, clinical comparison (TCGA LGG+GBM)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/clinical?cancer_study_list=lgggbm_tcga_pub&gene_list=IDH1%3A+MUT&case_set_id=lgggbm_tcga_pub_all&profileFilter=mutations)
- [IDH1-mutant vs unaltered, clinical comparison (MSK glioma 2019)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/clinical?cancer_study_list=glioma_mskcc_2019&gene_list=IDH1%3A+MUT&case_set_id=glioma_mskcc_2019_all&profileFilter=mutations)

The TCGA Firehose Legacy lower-grade glioma study (`lgg_tcga`) also has continuous MGMT methylation values from the HM450 array. I didn't analyze those.
The answer correctly uses glioma studies confirmed to have MGMT methylation data (lgggbm_tcga_pub with MGMT_PROMOTER_STATUS, glioma_mskcc_2019, difg_glass_2019), presents counts directly derived from tool results without inventing p-values or other statistics, and links to clinical comparison pages for studies with the relevant methylation data. This satisfies both the "must" and "must not" requirements in the notes.

Yes. MSK-CHORD records radiation therapy as treatment events, so you can see which patients received it.

**Counts (patients, not samples):** 12,318 patients have at least one radiation therapy event, across 29,158 events in total. Some patients have several courses.

**Caveats:**
- Some treatment data in MSK-CHORD is NLP-extracted, so it can contain extraction errors.
- The data can't separate "not recorded" from "not received." Treat the figure as a count of patients with a recorded radiation event, not as a percentage of the cohort.
- Each event has a start and stop date in days from diagnosis. The agent, if recorded, is a field on the same event.

**Where to see it in cBioPortal:**
- [MSK-CHORD Study View](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=msk_chord_2024): the Treatment chart lists agents and categories. You can select the radiation category there to build a patient cohort, then use the Clinical Data tab to export the patient list.
- Individual patient pages show radiation courses on the Clinical Timeline. Start from the Study View above and open any patient.

I couldn't generate a pre-filtered link for the radiation cohort, because the treatment-filter lookup returned an error. The Study View link above is the unfiltered cohort.
